A paired t-test comparing the post-hoc pleasantness ratings for the 2 voice identities was additionally used to validate the a priori voice selections as being significantly different in pleasantness.

Finally, we re-ran the 2 models tested in Experiment 1 to test for an internal replication of the core effects of enjoyment on comprehension and waiting outcomes.

For ease of interpretation, the effect sizes for categorical factors with 2 levels were computed as 2β for linear mixed-effects models and as exp(2β) for the logistic mixed-effects models.

Results & Discussion

The mean enjoyment of narratives heard in the higher-pleasantness voice was 5.53 (out of 9) and 5.36 for the lower-pleasantness voice. Mean comprehension accuracy was 69.4% (SD 16.5%), and participants waited to hear more of the book on a total of 226 trials (median 1 trial; range 0-17).

Voice identity (i.e. whether the voice was defined a priori as Higher Pleasantness or Lower Pleasantness) had a significant main effect on the enjoyment of a narrative (Estimate = 0.18 [0.06-0.30]; χ² = 8.79, p = .003), where participants reported greater enjoyment of excerpts narrated in the higher-pleasantness voice (Figure 2a). There was no significant main effect of voice identity on comprehension accuracy (OR = 0.87 [0.69-1.10]; χ² = 1.16, p = .282) or waiting (OR = 1.41 [0.77-2.56]; χ² = 1.35, p = .246).

Voice pleasantness (i.e. the post-hoc pleasantness rating given to the voice) also predicted enjoyment (Estimate = 0.01 [0.00-0.01]; χ² = 16.26, p < .001; Figure 3b), but had no effect on comprehension accuracy (OR = 1.00 [0.99-1.00]; χ² = 0.68, p = .410) or waiting (OR = 1.01 [0.99-1.03]; χ² = 1.34, p = .247).

Analysis of the post-hoc pleasantness ratings validated that the two selected voice identities were perceived as significantly different in pleasantness (Higher Pleasantness: 84.5/100; Lower Pleasantness: 60.0/100; t(79) = 8.93, p < .001, d = 1.00; Figure 2c). Further, the effects reported in Experiment 1 were replicated, where enjoyment significantly predicted both comprehension accuracy (OR = 1.15 [1.03-1.29]; χ² = 6.51, p = .011) and waiting (OR = 3.10 [2.19-4.39]; χ² = 33.07, p < .001). The findings of Experiment 2 provide evidence that the perceived qualities of a narrator's voice can influence the experience of an audio narrative. Specifically, in the context of narrative listening, a voice that was selected a priori to be of higher pleasantness for listening to audiobooks yielded greater enjoyment of spoken narratives than a voice of lower perceived pleasantness, a result that was also observed when considering the individual listeners' posthoc ratings of voice pleasantness (although potentially weakened by variability in the listeners' use of the pleasantness scale). This aligns with previous reports that first impressions of voices can impact listeners' decisions about what the voices say, for example the finding that listeners rate spoken statements as less credible when they are heard in foreign-accented speech (Lev-Ari & Keysar, 2010).

While highly significant, the magnitude of the effect of narrator on enjoyment was modest, yielding an overall mean increase of 0.17 (on a 1-9 scale) in the face of a much larger 24.5point difference (on a 0-100 scale) in the average perceived pleasantness of the two voice identities. We also found no evidence that the narrator's voice directly predicted other outcomes of narrative listening. Specifically the lack of effect on motivation to hear more indicates a possible dissociation of "liking" (as measured by enjoyment) and "wanting" (as measured by waiting) when it comes to narrator pleasantness (Berridge & Kringelbach, 2008).

Other properties of the narrator voice itself may also enhance the experience of spoken narratives, given the documented evidence for the perceptual and cognitive benefits of listening to familiar and personally-relevant voices. In Experiment 3, we therefore explored the effects of personally-familiar narrators on enjoyment and listening outcomes.

Experiment 3

Findings from familiar voice perception research have reported that speech heard in a familiar voice is better understood and distinctly encoded in the brain, particularly for personally familiar voices (Holmes & Johnsrude, 2021; Kanber, Lally, et al., 2025). Moreover, voices of personally-valued others, such as favourite celebrities and loved ones, engages reward and motivation systems (Abrams et al., 2013 (Abrams et al., , 2016 (Abrams et al., , 2019 (Abrams et al., , 2022;; Kanber, Roiser, et al., 2025; Seltzer et al., 2010 Seltzer et al., , 2012)). On this basis, we expected that narratives with highly personallyfamiliar and personally-relevant narrators should be enjoyed more, better understood, and more likely to engage onward listening, compared with narratives with unfamiliar narrators. We tested two specific familiar identities: the self and a personally familiar other. This approach allowed us to test whether the specific relationship to the voice influences the listening experiences and outcomes. Prior research on self-voice perception presents conflicting evidence regarding listeners' evaluations of their own voices: While some research has claimed that recordings of the self-voice are disliked relative to other voices (Maurer & Landis, 2009; Orepic et al., 2023), other studies have reported a self-voice preference (Hughes & Harrison, 2013; Peng et al., 2019 Peng et al., , 2020 Peng et al., , 2021;; Rosi et al., 2026). More relevant to the current study, however, recent work on the perception of cloned self-and familiar-othervoice samples showed that although listeners tend to evaluate a friend's cloned voice more positively than their own cloned voice, there is some degree at egotism at play because greater perceived similarity between the clone and the original speaker's "real" voice predicted higher ratings of warmth and competence for both the friend's voice and the self-voice (Rosi et al., 2025). Applying these recent findings to narrative listening in the current experiment, we expected to see main effects of voice identity (friend > self) and similarity on enjoyment.

Finally, we expected that listeners who reported a greater liking for the cloned versions of their own and their friend's voice would experience higher levels of enjoyment, similar to the effects of narrator pleasantness in Experiment 2.

Methods Participants

Two groups of participants were recruited to this experiment. The Experimental group included 25 pairs of people in personally-familiar relationships (e.g. romantic partners, friends), who signed up for a study on voice cloning in which they were aware that both their own and their friend's voices would be cloned. This sample size was determined based on the study's budget and timeframe. Recruitment was carried out via the XXXXX Psychology participant pool and through outreach to XXXX-affiliated groups and communities using email, social media, and personal contacts. Each recruited pair reported the duration of their relationship (from two months to over five years) and the frequency of their communication (ranging from daily to monthly). All participants were native English speakers, representing a range of self-identified accents such as British, North American, and South Asian. These participants attended an in-person voice recording session and completed a series of online testing sessions investigating their responses to the two (self, friend) recorded and cloned voice identities. The current experiment formed the last online session: due to some attrition across the sessions, 47 of the 50 participants in the Experimental group completed the narrative listening experiment (33 female, 12 male, 2 prefer not to say; mean age = 22.7 years, SD = 2.9 years). A Control group of 47 participants (32 female, 15 male; mean age = 33.8 years, SD = 7.7 years) was recruited to the experiment via Prolific.ac based on the following inclusion criteria: aged between 18-50 years old, with English as their first and primary language, based in England at the time of the experiment, and without hearing difficulties. We further required that participants had a minimum approval rating of 90% on Prolific and had made a minimum of 20 previous submissions on the platform. No participants were excluded. This study was not preregistered.

Materials

For this experiment, all 20 pairs of narrated book excerpts were separately generated in cloned voices of all 47 Experimental group participants, using the same audio processing methods as outlined for Experiment 1.

Design & Procedure

Each Experimental group participant completed 20 trials, where 10 narratives were presented in their own cloned voice, and 10 in their friend's cloned voice. Each Control group participant was randomly assigned to hear the same pair of cloned voices as one of the Experimental group participants, thus ensuring that the same 47 voice pairings were presented across the 2 groups. As in Experiment 2, each trial began with an onscreen label indicating whether the upcoming narrator would be Voice A or Voice B, and the assignment of two voices to Set A and Set B of the narratives was counterbalanced across participants. Delays following a "wait" decision were fixed at 4.5 seconds. After the main experimental task, all participants indicated which voice they preferred, and gave pleasantness ratings to each voice.

For the Experimental group only, we had additional relevant measures from an earlier session of the study in which they were presented with two short stories from Aesop's fables -The North Wind and the Sun and The Crow and the Pitcher -in both their own cloned voice and in their friend's cloned voice. After each story, the participant rated how much they liked the voice (liking; 0-100), and how much it sounded like them/their friend (similarity; 0-100). The mean scores were used in additional analyses of the narrative listening task data for the Experimental group participants only.

Data analysis

The overall approach matched that used in Experiments 1 and 2.

The enjoyment variable, the post-hoc voice pleasantness ratings (Control group only), and the familiarity and liking ratings (Experimental group only) were mean-centred across participants, and all trials where the participant reported having previously heard or read the book were also removed (10 trials in total, from 9 unique participants). Where analyses were run on the Experimental and Control groups separately, the enjoyment variable was accordingly centred within group. Random Analyses including all participants investigated the effect of voice familiarity on narrative listening by modelling the effect of listener group (Experimental, Control) on enjoyment (9), comprehension (10), and waiting (11). Here, we deviate from the analysis reported by Jones et al. (2025) when investigating reading enjoyment in adults with dyslexia, where they tested for a significant interaction of enjoyment with group on comprehension and waiting. Our reasoning is that we expected voice familiarity to enhance enjoyment of the narrative rather than interact with it, and on the basis that we expected Experimental and Control group listeners to engage with the task in a similar way. As Familiarity was a between-subjects factor, random slopes were not modelled by participant.

Analyses including only the Experimental group tested the interaction of voice identity (self, friend) with ratings of similarity and liking on book enjoyment (12), comprehension (13), and waiting ( 14):

Analyses including only the Control group re-ran models from Experiment 2 to test for an internal replication of the effect of voice pleasantness (0-100) on enjoyment (17), comprehension (18), and waiting (19):

We also re-ran the 2 models tested in Experiment 1, including all participants, to test for a second internal replication of the core effects of enjoyment on comprehension (18) and waiting (19) outcomes:

Results

& Discussion

The mean enjoyment of book excerpts was 5.82 (out of 9) for the Experimental group, who were familiar with the narrator's voice identities, and 5.19 for the Control group, who heard the same narrators as unfamiliar identities. Mean comprehension accuracy was 66.8% (SD = 16.4%), and participants waited to hear more of the book on a total of 172 trials (median 1 trial; range 0-17).

Analyses with all participants found a significant main effect of familiarity on enjoyment (Estimate = 0.64 [0.22-1.06]; χ² = 8.34, p = .004), where the listeners hearing the cloned narrators of familiar voice identities (Experimental group) reported greater enjoyment than participants who were unfamiliar with the cloned voice identities (Control group) (see Figure 4A). A second main effect associated familiar narrators with a greater likelihood of waiting to hear more of the book (OR = 2.04 [1.00-4.24]; χ² = 3.89, p = .049; Figure 4B). There was no main effect of familiarity on comprehension accuracy (OR = 0.94 [0.65-1.37]; χ² = 0.10, p = .751).

Although the Experimental participants were familiar with the narrator voice identities, having heard them in several previous sessions, we observed substantial variation in how these voices were perceived. Namely, participants rated the similarity of the cloned self-voice to their own "real" voice with values from 0-90 (out of 100; mean = 49.3; SD = 25.3) and the average rated similarity of the friend's cloned voice to their friend's "real" voice ranged from 0-98 (mean = 53.3; SD = 25.6). Participants rated their liking of the cloned self-voice from 5-100 (again, out of 100; mean = 61.5; SD = 19.8) and their average rated liking of the friend's cloned voice ranged from 2-92 (mean = 61.0; SD = 19.3). Within this, however, simple linear regression models showed that similarity ratings accounted for only 12.0% of the variance in liking ratings for the cloned self-voice (Estimate = 0.26 [0.05-0.48], p = .017), and 40.1% of the variance in liking of the friend's cloned voice (Estimate = 0.49 [0.31-0.67], p < .001), suggesting some independence of these two perceived qualities. There was no effect of the voice identity on either similarity (t(46) = 0.91, p = 0.367, d = 0.14) or liking ratings (t(46) = 0.16, p = 0.871, d = 0.02).

For the Experimental group, there was a significant interaction of voice identity (Self, Friend) with liking on enjoyment (Estimate = -0.02 [-0.04 -0.00], χ² = 4.21, p = .04), where there was a stronger positive relationship between liking and enjoyment for the self voice (Figure 4C). A weak effect of Similarity on waiting indicated that greater perceived similarity between the narrator and the "real" voice of self/friend predicted a greater likelihood of waiting to hear more (Estimate = 1.02 [1.00 -1.03], χ² = 3.87, p = .049). There were no other significant main effects or interactions effects implicating voice identity, similarity, and liking.

Examining the Control group, we replicated findings from Experiment 2 across a larger set of narrators: Voice pleasantness (using post-hoc ratings of the 2 narrator voices) significantly predicted enjoyment of narrated book excerpts (Estimate = 0.02 [0.01-0.03]; χ² = 10.27, p = .001), but had no effect on comprehension accuracy (OR = 0.99 [0.98-1.00]; χ² = 1.27, p = .260) or waiting (OR = 1.01 [0.99-1.02]; χ² = 0.95, p = .332).

Finally, the effects reported in Experiment 1 were again replicated across all participants, where enjoyment significantly predicted both comprehension accuracy (OR = 1.16 [1.06-1.27]; χ² = 9.15, p = .002) and waiting (OR = 2.66 [1.95-3.65]; χ² = 34.31, p < .001) The current results support our prediction that personally-familiar narration can enhance the experience of narrative listening. Specifically, we found that narratives were enjoyed more when listeners knew the people behind the narrators' voices, with an additional, weakly significant positive impact of familiarity on the likelihood of waiting to hear more. The effect of personal relevance per se has previously been shown to be very impactful on human processing of voices (Kanber, Roiser, et al., 2025; Payne et al., 2021). Here, the main comparison between the listener groups captured not only differences in the true familiarity of the narrator's voice, but also differences in the listeners' expectations, where the Experimental group anticipated hearing versions of their own and their friend's voices reading the books.

Familiarity increased mean enjoyment ratings by 0.63 (on a 1-9 scale), a larger effect compared with the boost of 0.17 for mean enjoyment ratings when listening to the higherpleasantness unfamiliar voice in Experiment 2.

Our analysis of the Experimental group allowed us to probe other aspects of how we experience voices in the context of (anticipated) familiarity. We found no main effect of voice identity (friend vs. self) on enjoyment, and found that perceived similarity to the "real" voice weakly predicted waiting, but not enjoyment. From these findings, we tentatively conclude that while proximity to the "true" sound of a familiar person alone may have little bearing on the hedonic response to narrative listening, it may still facilitate continued engagement with listening, perhaps via reduced listening effort in the presence of familiar vocal patterns (Herrmann & Johnsrude, 2020a) Participants' liking of cloned familiar voice identities was associated with enjoyment.

Specifically, an overall positive relationship between liking of the voice and enjoyment of the book was stronger for the cloned self-voice than for the friend's cloned voice. The main trend toward greater enjoyment with greater liking aligns with the predictive relationship between perceived voice pleasantness and enjoyment, observed in both Experiments 2 and 3. With the proviso that these data are sparse -since only one value was entered into the analysis per voice identity -the interactive relationship with voice identity raises interesting questions about how different personally-relevant voice identities might be experienced differently, and about self-voice preference. Peng et al. (2020) found that, in the context of multiple voices, listeners afford greater attractiveness ratings to their own voice compared with the voice of a friend. In contrast, other research has found that listeners afford more positive ratings of trait characteristics including attractiveness to their friend's (cloned) voice compared with their own (Rosi et al., 2025). Here, we show that, despite no overall difference in liking of the cloned self-voice and friend's voice, liking was more influential on the enjoyment of spoken narratives for the self-voice -at the very least, suggesting a special role in our perceptual experience for voices that are self-associated (Payne et al., 2021 (Payne et al., , 2024;; Rosi et al., 2024).

General Discussion

We have presented three experimental studies investigating the experiences and outcomes of narrative listening and how these might be modulated by the real or perceived qualities of a narrator's voice. We find highly robust evidence that effects previously observed in the context of reading generalise to listening to narrated texts: Across 3 internal replications and 3 participant groups, we find that greater enjoyment of a spoken book excerpt significantly predicts both increased likelihood of comprehending the excerpt and increased motivation to listen further. Using experiments manipulating the voice identities of book narrators, we further present 2 internal replications of the finding that a more pleasant-sounding unfamiliar narrator increases enjoyment of a narrative, and show that having prior familiarity with the narrator leads to greater enjoyment (and motivation) than a completely unfamiliar narrator's voice.

However, the beneficial impacts of a self-relevant narrator may be limited by the extent to which the voice is liked, and by its similarity to the listener's perceptual representation of the narrator.

The three experiments revealed dissociations between enjoyment, comprehension, and motivation/waiting in terms of their direct sensitivity to manipulations of the narrator voice.

These findings help to further characterise the mechanisms of narrative listening experiences and outcomes. For example, we consistently find that more positive evaluations of narrators predict greater enjoyment of narratives. However, these perceived narrator qualities did not predict comprehension of the narratives or the motivation to stay engaged with them. Thus, within a reward and motivation framework, what sounds "nice" has a consistent and apparently direct impact on hedonistic experience and "liking" (Berridge & Kringelbach, 2008) but only an indirect effect on other outcomes of listening (as demonstrated via 3 replications of enjoyment predicting both comprehension and waiting). In contrast, familiarity (Experiment 3) was predictive of both greater enjoyment and waiting to hear more of a narrative, while perceived similarity of the cloned narrator to the "real" voice of the cloned individual predicted greater waiting. These patterns of results support previous findings that familiar voices can have incentive value beyond -and independent of -their mere pleasantness, where listeners are willing to work harder (or, here, wait longer) to hear them (Kanber, Roiser, et al., 2025).

One note of caution for the understanding of our claims: We acknowledge that scales measuring "enjoyment", "pleasantness", and "liking" were used across our experiments, however we made no assumptions that these should tap into different underlying concepts for listeners. Instead, the crucial distinction for the interpretation of our results is that pleasantness and liking were rated in relation to listeners' experience of the voices being heard, but enjoyment was always rated in relation to the listeners' experience of the narrative. Thus, our repeated findings that greater pleasantness and liking were predictive of greater enjoyment reflect the contribution of the sound of a narrator's voice to the overall experience of the story being heard, and not merely the statistical relationship between different voice rating scales.

29

The absence of an effect of familiarity on comprehension (Experiment 3) constrains our speculation that information spoken in a familiar voice identity might be better understood via better encoding of the speech content. With the proviso that the familiar voices used here were imperfect clones that may not capture all the idiosyncrasies of a person's speaking style, our current interpretation of this null result is that any effect of familiarity on narrative comprehension may proceed indirectly, potentially via increased motivation leading to closer attentional engagement with known and liked voice identities. Future work in the domain of speech and hearing sciences might in turn consider the contributions of reward and motivation processes to familiar-voice benefits for speech intelligibility (Holmes et al., 2021; Kreitewolf et al., 2017; Nygaard et al., 1994; Nygaard & Pisoni, 1998; Yonan & Sommers, 2000; Barker & Newman, 2004; Domingo et al., 2019 Domingo et al., , 2020;; Holmes et al., 2018; Johnsrude et al., 2013; Newman & Evers, 2007; Souza et al., 2013 ).

In our study, we find evidence for correlational relationships between enjoyment, comprehension, and motivation during brief listening episodes. It will be crucially important to investigate how personalising narrative listening might causally affect engagement with spoken narratives over extended periods of listening -for example, an entire audiobook. In their Model of Listening Engagement (MoLE), Herrmann and Johnsrude (2020a) propose that engagement (recruitment of resources), motivation (willingness to recruit those resources), and experiences (enjoyment, boredom, effort) are strongly interrelated in narrative listening.

Specifically, while motivation can influence both engagement and experiences, the model proposes that experiences themselves can only directly modulate motivation (and not engagement). When considering our findings, it may be the case that narrator design can temporarily enhance the initial motivation to engage via the pleasant qualities of a particular voice (i.e. Experiences → Motivation → Engagement; our Experiment 2), but that over time the motivation toward a particular narrator becomes independent of voice quality (Motivation → Engagement & Experiences; our Experiment 3). The use of both subjective and objective measures of motivation, engagement, and experiences across longer narratives may allow for the causal mechanisms and timecourse underpinning narrator influences to be tracked and understood.

Our findings are highly relevant to questions around how reward and motivation can be harnessed to engage people with book language and its benefits for developing skills within and beyond language processing (Nation et al., 2022). Having shown that spoken narratives engage similar responses in relation to reward and motivation as reading texts, we have a solid evidence base to investigate whether listening to books can offer a viable alternative for people who report low reading engagement. For example, Jones et al. (2025) found that, for dyslexic readers, moment-to-moment reading enjoyment has a relatively weaker predictive effect on motivation and no predictive effect on comprehension. Although it is understood that difficulties with reading may co-occur with spoken language difficulties (Brady & Calcus, 2021; Li et al., 2023), taking book language into the audio modality may reveal greater similarities of effects across neurotypical and dyslexic listeners.

The current study has further indicated several possible avenues along which the experience of audiobook listening as an alternative route to book language might be enhanced, via the real or perceived properties of the narrator. Offering readers a choice of book genre or specific book title has been shown to increase reward and motivation for reading (Bains et al., 2025) -future work should address whether such benefits might be additive in combination with a choice of book narrator. It will also be important to address factors specific to audiobook listening: While a reader can imagine (or not) the narrator's and characters' voices in a written book, audiobook listening solidifies the relationship between the narrator and the text.

Experiments using auditory Stroop paradigms report slowed reaction times to incongruent pairings of speaker and speech, for example hearing a female-sounding voice say "father" rather than "mother" (Sommers & Danielson, 1999): Similarly, perceived mismatches between aspects of a heard voice identity and the expected or actual content of a narrative may disrupt enjoyment and other outcomes of audiobook listening. The rate of delivery of spoken narrative content will also likely have impacts on the listening experience -if too slow, it may diminish motivation relative to self-paced reading (see Experiment 1 of this paper), but if played too fast, the intelligibility of the book content will be compromised (Schlueter et al., 2014). Future experimental work should investigate the trade-offs between enjoyment and the quality of encoding in shaping narrative listening outcomes.

Some limitations of the current work must be acknowledged and addressed in future studies.

First, the current study used synthetic voice identities and text-to-speech synthesis to produce the spoken narratives heard by listeners. Although the kinds of cloned voices used in the current study have been shown in other work to be indistinguishable from authentic recordings of the same voice identities based on short word and sentence stimuli (and at least for unfamiliar listeners; Lavan et al., 2025; Rosi et al., 2025), in the context of narrative listening the text-to-speech synthesis will not have had the same style and quality of spoken delivery as might be expected from a professional human narrator. A second limitation to consider is that we presented familiar-voiced narrators via voice clones rather than authentic recordings of those familiar speakers. Familiar listeners are very sensitive to the lack of authenticity in the type of cloned audio we used here (Rosi et al., 2025), and our current listeners reported only moderate levels of perceived similarity on average between the cloned voices and their human counterparts. There is room for improvement in creating better likenesses through fine-tuned personalised cloning models or via voice conversion, which allows a pre-existing human voice recording to be modified to convey the vocal identity of another human (and thus could preserve a more appropriate spoken delivery than is available from fully text-to-speech synthesis).

Figure 1 Outline of trial structure. Expt = Experiment; 4 AFC = four-alternative forced choice task.

Figure 2

Figure 2 Results of Experiment 1. Greater enjoyment of a spoken narrative excerpt significantly predicted (a) the likelihood of correctly answering a comprehension question, and (b) the likelihood of waiting to hear a further excerpt.

Figure 3

Figure 3 Results of Experiment 2. (a) Spoken narrative excerpts read in the higherpleasantness voice were enjoyed significantly more than those in the lower-pleasantness voice. (b) Post-hoc ratings of voice pleasantness were significantly higher for the higherpleasantness voice than for the lower-pleasantness voice.

Figure 4

Figure 4 Results of Experiment 3. (a) Spoken narrative excerpts were enjoyed significantly more when narrated by the cloned voice of a familiar person (Experimental group) than by unfamiliar voices (Control group). (b) Participants were more likely to wait for more of the book when they were narrated by the cloned voice of a familiar person (Experimental group) than by unfamiliar voices (Control group) (c) Perceived similarity of cloned narrator voices to the "real" voice of the familiar person predicted the likelihood of waiting (Experimental group) (d) Liking of the narrator voice was more strongly predictive of enjoyment for the cloned self-voice than for the cloned voice of a friend (Experimental group).
